It’s mostly tied to work, I think—well, “work” is a little blurry because the experiment follows me home in my head. But the thing I came in about is that I can’t keep my mind on the actual experiment anymore. I’ll be in a grant call or a safety meeting, and then I hear the pump making a slightly different vibration, or a student makes some side comment, or I remember some weird peak from an old spectrum, and suddenly I’m chasing that for hours.

I was interested in science stuff, but the real problem I’m here about is this past seven months.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself getting intensely absorbed in specific interests or tasks outside of work, to the point of losing track of time or ignoring other responsibilities, or is this kind of intense focus limited to your work-related concerns?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly tied to work, I think—well, “work” is a little blurry because the experiment follows me home in my head. But the thing I came in about is that I can’t keep my mind on the actual experiment anymore. I’ll be in a grant call or a safety meeting, and then I hear the pump making a slightly different vibration, or a student makes some side comment, or I remember some weird peak from an old spectrum, and suddenly I’m chasing that for hours.\n\nAnd then the thing I was supposed to be doing—the meeting, the run, whatever everyone needed from me—just falls apart. It’s not like a hobby where I forget dinner because I’m painting or something. It’s more like these work-related details grab me and I can’t stay with the main thing.\n\nDoctor: Outside of work, do you find yourself starting tasks and then switching to something else before finishing, or having trouble staying focused on what you're supposed to be doing—like during conversations, reading, or household chores?\n\nPatient: Outside of work… I mean, I don’t think of it like chores so much. It’s more that the experiment just won’t stay in its lane, if that makes sense. I’ll be trying to do something normal, or even just talking to someone, and then I’ll remember that odd peak from an old spectrum, or I’ll think about whether the pump vibration meant something, and then I’m mentally gone.\n\nBut the clearest place it happens is still at work—grant calls, safety meetings, actual lab runs. I’m supposed to be following the main thing, and then one tiny detail grabs me, like a student’s side comment, and I’m off chasing it for hours. Then whatever everyone needed me to do just… falls apart.\n\nDoctor: When you're in the middle of a task at work, like a meeting or an experiment, do you find it extremely difficult to ignore minor details or distractions, even when you know they’re not important, to the point that they completely take over your focus?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s exactly it. I’ll know, in the moment, that the meeting is what I’m supposed to be doing, or the actual run is what matters, but then something tiny pulls me sideways.\n\nLike the pump will sound a little different, or a student will say something almost throwaway, or I’ll remember some odd peak from an old spectrum—and then I’m gone. Not physically, but my head is just off chasing that one thing for hours sometimes. I’ll be in a grant call or a safety meeting, and I notice the pump vibration is a little different, or a student makes some side comment, or I remember this odd peak from an old spectrum—and then suddenly I’m chasing that for hours. Meanwhile the thing everyone actually needed me to do just falls apart.\n\nDoctor: Has this difficulty with distractions and staying on task affected your personal relationships or caused you significant stress outside of work?\n\nPatient: It’s… yes, it’s stressful, but I keep circling back to work because that’s where it’s become impossible to ignore. I came in because I can’t keep my mind on the actual experiment anymore.\n\nI’ll be in a grant call or a safety meeting, and then there’s this tiny thing—the pump sounds different, or a student says something offhand, or I remember some strange peak from an old spectrum—and suddenly that’s all I can follow. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes. I mean, that’s the part that scares me. It’s not just me feeling scattered in my own head anymore.

My department has had to step in on decisions I used to just handle myself. Not even dramatic things, sometimes it’s scheduling, priorities, who needs to be where, what gets done first—and then I realize I’ve been focused on some tiny side issue and the main decision didn’t get made clearly.

And with the lab, it’s… I don’t want to sound careless, because I’ve always been careful. But the missed priorities and confused scheduling, and me not supervising people the way I should, it started putting the project at risk, and the safety expectations too. So yes, it’s definitely affecting my confidence. I feel like people are watching for where I’m going to drop the ball now.

I’m still on the pump vibration or the spectrum or whatever it was, and it’s exhausting.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that this pattern of getting sidetracked by small details has caused significant problems for you, such as missing deadlines, making errors at work, or feeling overwhelmed to the point it affects your confidence or ability to perform your job?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean, that’s the part that scares me. It’s not just me feeling scattered in my own head anymore.\n\nMy department has had to step in on decisions I used to just handle myself. Not even dramatic things, sometimes it’s scheduling, priorities, who needs to be where, what gets done first—and then I realize I’ve been focused on some tiny side issue and the main decision didn’t get made clearly.\n\nAnd with the lab, it’s… I don’t want to sound careless, because I’ve always been careful. But the missed priorities and confused scheduling, and me not supervising people the way I should, it started putting the project at risk, and the safety expectations too. So yes, it’s definitely affecting my confidence. I feel like people are watching for where I’m going to drop the ball now.\n\n[Last Question]\nWhen you get caught up in these details at work or at home, do you find it extremely difficult to switch your attention away from them, even if you know you should focus on something else or if it's affecting your responsibilities?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes. That’s exactly it, I think. I know I should stop, and I’ll even tell myself, “No, this is not the point right now,” but then I can’t let go of it.

Like in a grant call, everyone’s talking about timelines or money, and I’ll notice the pump vibration in the lab sounds just slightly wrong—except I’m not even supposed to be thinking about that right then—and suddenly I’m mentally gone. Or a student says something small, almost nothing, and I’m chasing that thread. Or I remember some odd peak from an old spectrum and it feels urgent, like I have to figure it out right now.

And then hours can go by. The actual experiment, or the decision people needed from me, just… falls apart because I couldn’t switch back. It’s embarrassing, because I can see it happening, but seeing it doesn’t seem to make me able to stop.

been going on for about seven months, since I took over the multi-site spectroscopy project.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 42 / physical_scientist / bachelors\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S039\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Has this difficulty with distractions and staying on task affected your personal relationships or caused you significant stress outside of work?\n\nPatient: It’s… yes, it’s stressful, but I keep circling back to work because that’s where it’s become impossible to ignore. I came in because I can’t keep my mind on the actual experiment anymore.\n\nI’ll be in a grant call or a safety meeting, and then there’s this tiny thing—the pump sounds different, or a student says something offhand, or I remember some strange peak from an old spectrum—and suddenly that’s all I can follow. I’m off chasing it for hours, and the thing everyone actually needed from me just falls apart.\n\nAnd then I carry that home in my head, so I’m not really present. I’m still on the pump vibration or the spectrum or whatever it was, and it’s exhausting.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that this pattern of getting sidetracked by small details has caused significant problems for you, such as missing deadlines, making errors at work, or feeling overwhelmed to the point it affects your confidence or ability to perform your job?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean, that’s the part that scares me. It’s not just me feeling scattered in my own head anymore.\n\nMy department has had to step in on decisions I used to just handle myself. Not even dramatic things, sometimes it’s scheduling, priorities, who needs to be where, what gets done first—and then I realize I’ve been focused on some tiny side issue and the main decision didn’t get made clearly.\n\nAnd with the lab, it’s… I don’t want to sound careless, because I’ve always been careful. But the missed priorities and confused scheduling, and me not supervising people the way I should, it started putting the project at risk, and the safety expectations too. So yes, it’s definitely affecting my confidence. I feel like people are watching for where I’m going to drop the ball now.\n\nDoctor: When you get caught up in these details at work or at home, do you find it extremely difficult to switch your attention away from them, even if you know you should focus on something else or if it's affecting your responsibilities?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s exactly it, I think. I know I should stop, and I’ll even tell myself, “No, this is not the point right now,” but then I can’t let go of it.\n\nLike in a grant call, everyone’s talking about timelines or money, and I’ll notice the pump vibration in the lab sounds just slightly wrong—except I’m not even supposed to be thinking about that right then—and suddenly I’m mentally gone. Or a student says something small, almost nothing, and I’m chasing that thread. Or I remember some odd peak from an old spectrum and it feels urgent, like I have to figure it out right now.\n\nAnd then hours can go by. The actual experiment, or the decision people needed from me, just… falls apart because I couldn’t switch back. It’s embarrassing, because I can see it happening, but seeing it doesn’t seem to make me able to stop.\n\n[Last Question]\nCan you recall a time when you were so focused on a specific detail or task that you lost track of time or failed to notice what was going on around you, even when there were other important things happening that required your attention?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes. The grant call is probably the cleanest example, although “clean” is the wrong word because it wasn’t clean at all.

Everyone was talking about timelines and what we could realistically deliver, and I was supposed to be tracking the experiment plan, but I noticed this pump vibration in the background. It was just slightly different. And then I couldn’t leave it alone. I started thinking about whether it matched something from a previous run, and then that reminded me of an odd peak in an old spectrum, and then I was digging through that in my head instead of listening.

By the time I sort of came back, I realized I’d missed what people were asking me. Hours can go like that. A student can make some small side comment, or I see one detail that feels wrong, and suddenly I’m chasing that while the thing everyone actually needed from me just falls apart. It’s awful, because it isn’t that I don’t know there are more important things happening. I do know. I just can’t seem to stay with them.

Yes. Nearly impossible is… honestly, that’s what it feels like.

I came in because I can’t keep my mind on the actual experiment anymore. I’ll be in a grant call or a safety meeting, and I know I’m supposed to be following the main issue, but then I notice the pump vibration, or a student makes some side comment, or I remember an odd peak from an old spectrum, and suddenly my brain just goes there.

It’s more like these work-related details grab me and I can’t stay with the main thing.\n\nDoctor: Outside of work, do you find yourself starting tasks and then switching to something else before finishing, or having trouble staying focused on what you're supposed to be doing—like during conversations, reading, or household chores?\n\nPatient: Outside of work… I mean, I don’t think of it like chores so much. It’s more that the experiment just won’t stay in its lane, if that makes sense. I’ll be trying to do something normal, or even just talking to someone, and then I’ll remember that odd peak from an old spectrum, or I’ll think about whether the pump vibration meant something, and then I’m mentally gone.\n\nBut the clearest place it happens is still at work—grant calls, safety meetings, actual lab runs. I’m supposed to be following the main thing, and then one tiny detail grabs me, like a student’s side comment, and I’m off chasing it for hours. Then whatever everyone needed me to do just… falls apart.\n\nDoctor: When you're in the middle of a task at work, like a meeting or an experiment, do you find it extremely difficult to ignore minor details or distractions, even when you know they’re not important, to the point that they completely take over your focus?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s exactly it. I’ll know, in the moment, that the meeting is what I’m supposed to be doing, or the actual run is what matters, but then something tiny pulls me sideways.\n\nLike the pump will sound a little different, or a student will say something almost throwaway, or I’ll remember some odd peak from an old spectrum—and then I’m gone. Not physically, but my head is just off chasing that one thing for hours sometimes. And meanwhile the grant call, the safety meeting, the experiment everyone is waiting on… it just falls apart because I can’t keep my mind on the main thing.\n\nDoctor: Outside of work, do you have trouble finishing tasks like reading an article, following a conversation, or completing a project because your mind drifts or gets caught up in small details?\n\nPatient: Outside of work… sometimes, yes, but it’s hard for me to separate because work sort of follows me around. Like I’ll be reading something completely unrelated, or someone’s talking to me, and then my brain hooks onto some tiny thing from the lab.\n\nBut the reason I came in is really that I can’t keep my mind on the actual experiment anymore. I’ll be in a grant call or a safety meeting, and I notice the pump vibration is a little different, or a student makes some side comment, or I remember this odd peak from an old spectrum—and then suddenly I’m chasing that for hours. Meanwhile the thing everyone actually needed me to do just falls apart.\n\nDoctor: Has this difficulty with distractions and staying on task affected your personal relationships or caused you significant stress outside of work?\n\nPatient: It’s… yes, it’s stressful, but I keep circling back to work because that’s where it’s become impossible to ignore. I came in because I can’t keep my mind on the actual experiment anymore.\n\nI’ll be in a grant call or a safety meeting, and then there’s this tiny thing—the pump sounds different, or a student says something offhand, or I remember some strange peak from an old spectrum—and suddenly that’s all I can follow. I’m off chasing it for hours, and the thing everyone actually needed from me just falls apart.\n\nAnd then I carry that home in my head, so I’m not really present. I’m still on the pump vibration or the spectrum or whatever it was, and it’s exhausting.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that this pattern of getting sidetracked by small details has caused significant problems for you, such as missing deadlines, making errors at work, or feeling overwhelmed to the point it affects your confidence or ability to perform your job?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean, that’s the part that scares me. It’s not just me feeling scattered in my own head anymore.\n\nMy department has had to step in on decisions I used to just handle myself. Yes… mostly it’s shown up through work, but it spills over because work is where most of my relationships are, if that makes sense. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, that’s… that’s pretty much it. It isn’t just that I notice the thing and move on. I’ll be in a grant call or a safety meeting, and I know the actual experiment plan is what matters, but then there’s this pump vibration, or someone says something offhand, or I remember that old spectrum peak, and my mind just grabs it.

And then I keep circling it. Like if I can just figure out why it sounds different, or what that peak meant, then I can come back—but I don’t come back. Hours can disappear, and meanwhile the thing everyone needed from me is falling apart. It feels ridiculous because part of me knows it’s not the most important thing right then, but I still can’t seem to stop chasing it.

Nearly impossible is… honestly, that’s what it feels like.\n\nI came in because I can’t keep my mind on the actual experiment anymore. I’ll be in a grant call or a safety meeting, and I know I’m supposed to be following the main issue, but then I notice the pump vibration, or a student makes some side comment, or I remember an odd peak from an old spectrum, and suddenly my brain just goes there.\n\nAnd it’s not a quick thought. It turns into this whole chase, like I have to solve that one thing right then, even though everyone else needs me on the actual decision. Hours can go by, and then the important thing has fallen apart. It makes me feel like I’m not in control of my own attention.\n\nDoctor: Has this pattern of getting sidetracked by small details caused problems in your relationships or other areas of your life beyond work, such as with family, friends, or in social situations?\n\nPatient: Yes… mostly it’s shown up through work, but it spills over because work is where most of my relationships are, if that makes sense. My department has had to step in on decisions I used to handle myself. Not just small things—priorities I missed, scheduling getting confused, supervision with students getting strained.\n\nAnd then it becomes awkward with people, because they’re trying to trust me and I’m off chasing some tiny detail, and meanwhile the project plan is slipping. There were also lab safety expectations that people started worrying I wasn’t keeping on top of, which is… that’s hard to say out loud.\n\nI mean, I used to be the person people came to for those decisions. Now there’s this feeling that others have to check behind me or take over before something important gets missed. That has definitely affected how people relate to me.\n\nDoctor: When you get stuck on these small details, like the pump vibration or the old spectrum peak, do you feel like you have to keep thinking about them over and over, almost like you can't stop unless you figure them out completely, even if you know they're not that important?\n\nPatient: Yes, that’s… that’s pretty much it. It isn’t just that I notice the thing and move on. I’ll be in a grant call or a safety meeting, and I know the actual experiment plan is what matters, but then there’s this pump vibration, or someone says something offhand, or I remember that old spectrum peak, and my mind just grabs it.\n\nAnd then I keep circling it. Like if I can just figure out why it sounds different, or what that peak meant, then I can come back—but I don’t come back. Hours can disappear, and meanwhile the thing everyone needed from me is falling apart. It feels ridiculous because part of me knows it’s not the most important thing right then, but I still can’t seem to stop chasing it.\n\n[Last Question]\nWhen you get stuck on these small details like the pump vibration or an old spectrum peak, do you feel like you have to keep thinking about them over and over, almost like you can't stop unless you figure them out completely, even if you know they're not that important?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, and I know I already keep coming back to that, but that’s exactly the problem—I come in because I can’t keep my mind on the actual experiment anymore.

Like during a grant call, or a safety meeting, everyone’s talking about something important, and then I hear the pump vibrating differently, or a student says one little side thing, or I remember some odd peak in an old spectrum from months ago, and suddenly that’s where I am. I’m not really in the room anymore, mentally.

And I tell myself, okay, just note it and come back, but I don’t. I start chasing it, turning it over, trying to make it make sense, and then hours can go by. Meanwhile the thing everyone actually needed me for is just… falling apart. It’s embarrassing, because I know it shouldn’t matter that much right then, but it feels like I can’t let it go until I’ve figured it out.

I’m off chasing it for hours, and the thing everyone actually needed from me just falls apart.\n\nAnd then I carry that home in my head, so I’m not really present. I’m still on the pump vibration or the spectrum or whatever it was, and it’s exhausting.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that this pattern of getting sidetracked by small details has caused significant problems for you, such as missing deadlines, making errors at work, or feeling overwhelmed to the point it affects your confidence or ability to perform your job?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean, that’s the part that scares me. It’s not just me feeling scattered in my own head anymore.\n\nMy department has had to step in on decisions I used to just handle myself. Meanwhile the thing everyone actually needed me to do just falls apart.\n\nDoctor: Has this difficulty with distractions and staying on task affected your personal relationships or caused you significant stress outside of work?\n\nPatient: It’s… yes, it’s stressful, but I keep circling back to work because that’s where it’s become impossible to ignore. I came in because I can’t keep my mind on the actual experiment anymore.\n\nI’ll be in a grant call or a safety meeting, and then there’s this tiny thing—the pump sounds different, or a student says something offhand, or I remember some strange peak from an old spectrum—and suddenly that’s all I can follow. I’m off chasing it for hours, and the thing everyone actually needed from me just falls apart.\n\nAnd then I carry that home in my head, so I’m not really present. I’m still on the pump vibration or the spectrum or whatever it was, and it’s exhausting.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that this pattern of getting sidetracked by small details has caused significant problems for you, such as missing deadlines, making errors at work, or feeling overwhelmed to the point it affects your confidence or ability to perform your job?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean, that’s the part that scares me. It’s not just me feeling scattered in my own head anymore.\n\nMy department has had to step in on decisions I used to just handle myself. I feel like people are watching for where I’m going to drop the ball now.\n\nDoctor: When you get caught up in these details at work or at home, do you find it extremely difficult to switch your attention away from them, even if you know you should focus on something else or if it's affecting your responsibilities?\n\nPatient: Yes. I know I should stop, and I’ll even tell myself, “No, this is not the point right now,” but then I can’t let go of it.\n\nLike in a grant call, everyone’s talking about timelines or money, and I’ll notice the pump vibration in the lab sounds just slightly wrong—except I’m not even supposed to be thinking about that right then—and suddenly I’m mentally gone. It was just slightly different. And then I couldn’t leave it alone. I started thinking about whether it matched something from a previous run, and then that reminded me of an odd peak in an old spectrum, and then I was digging through that in my head instead of listening.\n\nBy the time I sort of came back, I realized I’d missed what people were asking me. Yes, but it’s not always the useful kind of absorbed. I mean, if it’s something that catches me, I can disappear into it for hours. Like I came in because I can’t keep my mind on the actual experiment anymore.

I feel like people are watching for where I’m going to drop the ball now.\n\nDoctor: When you get caught up in these details at work or at home, do you find it extremely difficult to switch your attention away from them, even if you know you should focus on something else or if it's affecting your responsibilities?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s exactly it, I think. I know I should stop, and I’ll even tell myself, “No, this is not the point right now,” but then I can’t let go of it.\n\nLike in a grant call, everyone’s talking about timelines or money, and I’ll notice the pump vibration in the lab sounds just slightly wrong—except I’m not even supposed to be thinking about that right then—and suddenly I’m mentally gone. Or a student says something small, almost nothing, and I’m chasing that thread. Or I remember some odd peak from an old spectrum and it feels urgent, like I have to figure it out right now.\n\nAnd then hours can go by. The actual experiment, or the decision people needed from me, just… falls apart because I couldn’t switch back. It’s embarrassing, because I can see it happening, but seeing it doesn’t seem to make me able to stop.\n\nDoctor: Can you recall a time when you were so focused on a specific detail or task that you lost track of time or failed to notice what was going on around you, even when there were other important things happening that required your attention?\n\nPatient: Yes. The grant call is probably the cleanest example, although “clean” is the wrong word because it wasn’t clean at all.\n\nEveryone was talking about timelines and what we could realistically deliver, and I was supposed to be tracking the experiment plan, but I noticed this pump vibration in the background. It was just slightly different. And then I couldn’t leave it alone. I started thinking about whether it matched something from a previous run, and then that reminded me of an odd peak in an old spectrum, and then I was digging through that in my head instead of listening.\n\nBy the time I sort of came back, I realized I’d missed what people were asking me. Hours can go like that. A student can make some small side comment, or I see one detail that feels wrong, and suddenly I’m chasing that while the thing everyone actually needed from me just falls apart. It’s awful, because it isn’t that I don’t know there are more important things happening. I do know. I just can’t seem to stay with them.\n\nDoctor: When you're stuck focusing on a minor detail, like the pump vibration or an old spectrum peak, do you find it nearly impossible to shift your mind to a different task or topic, even when you're aware that you need to?\n\nPatient: Yes. Nearly impossible is… honestly, that’s what it feels like.\n\nI came in because I can’t keep my mind on the actual experiment anymore. I’ll be in a grant call or a safety meeting, and I know I’m supposed to be following the main issue, but then I notice the pump vibration, or a student makes some side comment, or I remember an odd peak from an old spectrum, and suddenly my brain just goes there.\n\nAnd it’s not a quick thought. It turns into this whole chase, like I have to solve that one thing right then, even though everyone else needs me on the actual decision. Hours can go by, and then the important thing has fallen apart. It makes me feel like I’m not in control of my own attention.\n\nDoctor: Has this pattern of getting sidetracked by small details caused problems in your relationships or other areas of your life beyond work, such as with family, friends, or in social situations?\n\nPatient: Yes… mostly it’s shown up through work, but it spills over because work is where most of my relationships are, if that makes sense. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I mean, it’s not just work stress, though work is where everyone sees it. For months I’ve had this feeling like the lab is finally moving at my speed, like everything is sort of charged or electrified, if that makes sense.

And when people keep up with me, it feels great—like finally, yes, this is how fast it should have been going. But if someone questions the plan, or asks me to slow down and go back over something, I get… sharp. Too sharp. I snap at them. I’ve noticed students don’t come by my office the way they used to, which is embarrassing, because I don’t think I’m trying to scare them. It just feels intolerable in the moment, like they’re putting brakes on something that’s finally working.

But the thing I came in about is that I can’t keep my mind on the actual experiment anymore. It’s more that the experiment just won’t stay in its lane, if that makes sense. I’ll be trying to do something normal, or even just talking to someone, and then I’ll remember that odd peak from an old spectrum, or I’ll think about whether the pump vibration meant something, and then I’m mentally gone.\n\nBut the clearest place it happens is still at work—grant calls, safety meetings, actual lab runs. I’m supposed to be following the main thing, and then one tiny detail grabs me, like a student’s side comment, and I’m off chasing it for hours. I’ll know, in the moment, that the meeting is what I’m supposed to be doing, or the actual run is what matters, but then something tiny pulls me sideways.\n\nLike the pump will sound a little different, or a student will say something almost throwaway, or I’ll remember some odd peak from an old spectrum—and then I’m gone. Not physically, but my head is just off chasing that one thing for hours sometimes. I’ll be in a grant call or a safety meeting, and I notice the pump vibration is a little different, or a student makes some side comment, or I remember this odd peak from an old spectrum—and then suddenly I’m chasing that for hours. Meanwhile the thing everyone actually needed me to do just falls apart.\n\nDoctor: Has this difficulty with distractions and staying on task affected your personal relationships or caused you significant stress outside of work?\n\nPatient: It’s… yes, it’s stressful, but I keep circling back to work because that’s where it’s become impossible to ignore. I came in because I can’t keep my mind on the actual experiment anymore.\n\nI’ll be in a grant call or a safety meeting, and then there’s this tiny thing—the pump sounds different, or a student says something offhand, or I remember some strange peak from an old spectrum—and suddenly that’s all I can follow. I’m off chasing it for hours, and the thing everyone actually needed from me just falls apart.\n\nAnd then I carry that home in my head, so I’m not really present. I’m still on the pump vibration or the spectrum or whatever it was, and it’s exhausting.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that this pattern of getting sidetracked by small details has caused significant problems for you, such as missing deadlines, making errors at work, or feeling overwhelmed to the point it affects your confidence or ability to perform your job?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean, that’s the part that scares me. It’s not just me feeling scattered in my own head anymore.\n\nMy department has had to step in on decisions I used to just handle myself. Not even dramatic things, sometimes it’s scheduling, priorities, who needs to be where, what gets done first—and then I realize I’ve been focused on some tiny side issue and the main decision didn’t get made clearly.\n\nAnd with the lab, it’s… I don’t want to sound careless, because I’ve always been careful. But the missed priorities and confused scheduling, and me not supervising people the way I should, it started putting the project at risk, and the safety expectations too. So yes, it’s definitely affecting my confidence. I feel like people are watching for where I’m going to drop the ball now.\n\nDoctor: When you get caught up in these details at work or at home, do you find it extremely difficult to switch your attention away from them, even if you know you should focus on something else or if it's affecting your responsibilities?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s exactly it, I think. I know I should stop, and I’ll even tell myself, “No, this is not the point right now,” but then I can’t let go of it.\n\nLike in a grant call, everyone’s talking about timelines or money, and I’ll notice the pump vibration in the lab sounds just slightly wrong—except I’m not even supposed to be thinking about that right then—and suddenly I’m mentally gone. Or a student says something small, almost nothing, and I’m chasing that thread. Or I remember some odd peak from an old spectrum and it feels urgent, like I have to figure it out right now.\n\nAnd then hours can go by. The actual experiment, or the decision people needed from me, just… falls apart because I couldn’t switch back. It’s embarrassing, because I can see it happening, but seeing it doesn’t seem to make me able to stop.\n\nDoctor: Can you recall a time when you were so focused on a specific detail or task that you lost track of time or failed to notice what was going on around you, even when there were other important things happening that required your attention?\n\nPatient: Yes. The grant call is probably the cleanest example, although “clean” is the wrong word because it wasn’t clean at all.\n\nEveryone was talking about timelines and what we could realistically deliver, and I was supposed to be tracking the experiment plan, but I noticed this pump vibration in the background. It was just slightly different. And then I couldn’t leave it alone. I started thinking about whether it matched something from a previous run, and then that reminded me of an odd peak in an old spectrum, and then I was digging through that in my head instead of listening.\n\nBy the time I sort of came back, I realized I’d missed what people were asking me. Hours can go like that. A student can make some small side comment, or I see one detail that feels wrong, and suddenly I’m chasing that while the thing everyone actually needed from me just falls apart. It’s awful, because it isn’t that I don’t know there are more important things happening. I do know. I just can’t seem to stay with them.\n\nDoctor: When you're stuck focusing on a minor detail, like the pump vibration or an old spectrum peak, do you find it nearly impossible to shift your mind to a different task or topic, even when you're aware that you need to?\n\nPatient: Yes. Nearly impossible is… honestly, that’s what it feels like.\n\nI came in because I can’t keep my mind on the actual experiment anymore. I’ll be in a grant call or a safety meeting, and I know I’m supposed to be following the main issue, but then I notice the pump vibration, or a student makes some side comment, or I remember an odd peak from an old spectrum, and suddenly my brain just goes there.\n\nAnd it’s not a quick thought. It turns into this whole chase, like I have to solve that one thing right then, even though everyone else needs me on the actual decision. Hours can go by, and then the important thing has fallen apart. It makes me feel like I’m not in control of my own attention.\n\nDoctor: Has this pattern of getting sidetracked by small details caused problems in your relationships or other areas of your life beyond work, such as with family, friends, or in social situations?\n\nPatient: Yes… mostly it’s shown up through work, but it spills over because work is where most of my relationships are, if that makes sense. I know I should stop, and I’ll even tell myself, “No, this is not the point right now,” but then I can’t let go of it.\n\nLike in a grant call, everyone’s talking about timelines or money, and I’ll notice the pump vibration in the lab sounds just slightly wrong—except I’m not even supposed to be thinking about that right then—and suddenly I’m mentally gone. Or I remember some odd peak from an old spectrum and it feels urgent, like I have to figure it out right now.\n\nAnd then hours can go by. The actual experiment, or the decision people needed from me, just… falls apart because I couldn’t switch back. It’s embarrassing, because I can see it happening, but seeing it doesn’t seem to make me able to stop.\n\nDoctor: Can you recall a time when you were so focused on a specific detail or task that you lost track of time or failed to notice what was going on around you, even when there were other important things happening that required your attention?\n\nPatient: Yes. The grant call is probably the cleanest example, although “clean” is the wrong word because it wasn’t clean at all.\n\nEveryone was talking about timelines and what we could realistically deliver, and I was supposed to be tracking the experiment plan, but I noticed this pump vibration in the background. I’m not sure I’d call it racing, exactly. I mean… people have said I’m hard to follow sometimes, but that could just be because I’m trying to explain the whole picture and they want the short version.

It’s embarrassing, because I can see it happening, but seeing it doesn’t seem to make me able to stop.\n\nDoctor: Can you recall a time when you were so focused on a specific detail or task that you lost track of time or failed to notice what was going on around you, even when there were other important things happening that required your attention?\n\nPatient: Yes. The grant call is probably the cleanest example, although “clean” is the wrong word because it wasn’t clean at all.\n\nEveryone was talking about timelines and what we could realistically deliver, and I was supposed to be tracking the experiment plan, but I noticed this pump vibration in the background. It was just slightly different. And then I couldn’t leave it alone. I started thinking about whether it matched something from a previous run, and then that reminded me of an odd peak in an old spectrum, and then I was digging through that in my head instead of listening.\n\nBy the time I sort of came back, I realized I’d missed what people were asking me. Hours can go like that. A student can make some small side comment, or I see one detail that feels wrong, and suddenly I’m chasing that while the thing everyone actually needed from me just falls apart. It’s awful, because it isn’t that I don’t know there are more important things happening. I do know. I just can’t seem to stay with them.\n\nDoctor: When you're stuck focusing on a minor detail, like the pump vibration or an old spectrum peak, do you find it nearly impossible to shift your mind to a different task or topic, even when you're aware that you need to?\n\nPatient: Yes. Nearly impossible is… honestly, that’s what it feels like.\n\nI came in because I can’t keep my mind on the actual experiment anymore. I’ll be in a grant call or a safety meeting, and I know I’m supposed to be following the main issue, but then I notice the pump vibration, or a student makes some side comment, or I remember an odd peak from an old spectrum, and suddenly my brain just goes there.\n\nAnd it’s not a quick thought. It turns into this whole chase, like I have to solve that one thing right then, even though everyone else needs me on the actual decision. Hours can go by, and then the important thing has fallen apart. It makes me feel like I’m not in control of my own attention.\n\nDoctor: Has this pattern of getting sidetracked by small details caused problems in your relationships or other areas of your life beyond work, such as with family, friends, or in social situations?\n\nPatient: Yes… mostly it’s shown up through work, but it spills over because work is where most of my relationships are, if that makes sense. My department has had to step in on decisions I used to handle myself. Not just small things—priorities I missed, scheduling getting confused, supervision with students getting strained.\n\nAnd then it becomes awkward with people, because they’re trying to trust me and I’m off chasing some tiny detail, and meanwhile the project plan is slipping. There were also lab safety expectations that people started worrying I wasn’t keeping on top of, which is… that’s hard to say out loud.\n\nI mean, I used to be the person people came to for those decisions. Now there’s this feeling that others have to check behind me or take over before something important gets missed. That has definitely affected how people relate to me.\n\nDoctor: When you get stuck on these small details, like the pump vibration or the old spectrum peak, do you feel like you have to keep thinking about them over and over, almost like you can't stop unless you figure them out completely, even if you know they're not that important?\n\nPatient: Yes, that’s… that’s pretty much it. It isn’t just that I notice the thing and move on. I’ll be in a grant call or a safety meeting, and I know the actual experiment plan is what matters, but then there’s this pump vibration, or someone says something offhand, or I remember that old spectrum peak, and my mind just grabs it.\n\nAnd then I keep circling it. Like if I can just figure out why it sounds different, or what that peak meant, then I can come back—but I don’t come back. Hours can disappear, and meanwhile the thing everyone needed from me is falling apart. It feels ridiculous because part of me knows it’s not the most important thing right then, but I still can’t seem to stop chasing it.\n\nDoctor: When you get stuck on these small details like the pump vibration or an old spectrum peak, do you feel like you have to keep thinking about them over and over, almost like you can't stop unless you figure them out completely, even if you know they're not that important?\n\nPatient: Yes, and I know I already keep coming back to that, but that’s exactly the problem—I come in because I can’t keep my mind on the actual experiment anymore.\n\nLike during a grant call, or a safety meeting, everyone’s talking about something important, and then I hear the pump vibrating differently, or a student says one little side thing, or I remember some odd peak in an old spectrum from months ago, and suddenly that’s where I am. I’m not really in the room anymore, mentally.\n\nAnd I tell myself, okay, just note it and come back, but I don’t. I start chasing it, turning it over, trying to make it make sense, and then hours can go by. Meanwhile the thing everyone actually needed me for is just… falling apart. It’s embarrassing, because I know it shouldn’t matter that much right then, but it feels like I can’t let it go until I’ve figured it out.\n\nDoctor: When you're working on a task or problem that really interests you, do you find yourself becoming so absorbed in it that you lose track of time or neglect other things, even if they’re important?\n\nPatient: Yes, but it’s not always the useful kind of absorbed. I start chasing that one thing, trying to figure it out.\n\nAnd then hours pass, and the actual task—the experiment plan, the decision, whatever everyone was waiting on—has fallen apart. It’s not like I’m choosing the important thing and focusing well. It’s more like my attention latches onto the wrong thing and won’t let go.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any problems with your mood, sleep, or general sense of well-being that aren't just related to the stress at work?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, it’s not just work stress, though work is where everyone sees it. For months I’ve had this feeling like the lab is finally moving at my speed, like everything is sort of charged or electrified, if that makes sense.\n\nAnd when people keep up with me, it feels great—like finally, yes, this is how fast it should have been going. But if someone questions the plan, or asks me to slow down and go back over something, I get… sharp. Too sharp. I snap at them. I’ve noticed students don’t come by my office the way they used to, which is embarrassing, because I don’t think I’m trying to scare them. It just feels intolerable in the moment, like they’re putting brakes on something that’s finally working.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your thoughts racing, or feeling like you need to keep talking because your mind is moving so fast?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it racing, exactly. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes—at the time it feels completely sensible. Like, if the pump vibration changes, that could mean something about the setup, or contamination, or a timing issue, and suddenly that feels more urgent than whatever the meeting was supposed to be about.

I’m off chasing it for hours, and the thing everyone actually needed from me just falls apart.\n\nAnd then I carry that home in my head, so I’m not really present. I mean, that’s the part that scares me. It’s not just me feeling scattered in my own head anymore.\n\nMy department has had to step in on decisions I used to just handle myself. It turns into this whole chase, like I have to solve that one thing right then, even though everyone else needs me on the actual decision. Hours can go by, and then the important thing has fallen apart. Not physically, but my head is just off chasing that one thing for hours sometimes. And meanwhile the grant call, the safety meeting, the experiment everyone is waiting on… it just falls apart because I can’t keep my mind on the main thing.\n\nDoctor: Outside of work, do you have trouble finishing tasks like reading an article, following a conversation, or completing a project because your mind drifts or gets caught up in small details?\n\nPatient: Outside of work… sometimes, yes, but it’s hard for me to separate because work sort of follows me around. I’ll be in a grant call or a safety meeting, and I notice the pump vibration is a little different, or a student makes some side comment, or I remember this odd peak from an old spectrum—and then suddenly I’m chasing that for hours. I’ll know, in the moment, that the meeting is what I’m supposed to be doing, or the actual run is what matters, but then something tiny pulls me sideways.\n\nLike the pump will sound a little different, or a student will say something almost throwaway, or I’ll remember some odd peak from an old spectrum—and then I’m gone. Not physically, but my head is just off chasing that one thing for hours sometimes. And meanwhile the grant call, the safety meeting, the experiment everyone is waiting on… it just falls apart because I can’t keep my mind on the main thing.\n\nDoctor: Outside of work, do you have trouble finishing tasks like reading an article, following a conversation, or completing a project because your mind drifts or gets caught up in small details?\n\nPatient: Outside of work… sometimes, yes, but it’s hard for me to separate because work sort of follows me around. Like I’ll be reading something completely unrelated, or someone’s talking to me, and then my brain hooks onto some tiny thing from the lab.\n\nBut the reason I came in is really that I can’t keep my mind on the actual experiment anymore. I’ll be in a grant call or a safety meeting, and I notice the pump vibration is a little different, or a student makes some side comment, or I remember this odd peak from an old spectrum—and then suddenly I’m chasing that for hours. Meanwhile the thing everyone actually needed me to do just falls apart.\n\nDoctor: Has this difficulty with distractions and staying on task affected your personal relationships or caused you significant stress outside of work?\n\nPatient: It’s… yes, it’s stressful, but I keep circling back to work because that’s where it’s become impossible to ignore. I came in because I can’t keep my mind on the actual experiment anymore.\n\nI’ll be in a grant call or a safety meeting, and then there’s this tiny thing—the pump sounds different, or a student says something offhand, or I remember some strange peak from an old spectrum—and suddenly that’s all I can follow. I’m off chasing it for hours, and the thing everyone actually needed from me just falls apart.\n\nAnd then I carry that home in my head, so I’m not really present. I’m still on the pump vibration or the spectrum or whatever it was, and it’s exhausting.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that this pattern of getting sidetracked by small details has caused significant problems for you, such as missing deadlines, making errors at work, or feeling overwhelmed to the point it affects your confidence or ability to perform your job?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean, that’s the part that scares me. It’s not just me feeling scattered in my own head anymore.\n\nMy department has had to step in on decisions I used to just handle myself. Not even dramatic things, sometimes it’s scheduling, priorities, who needs to be where, what gets done first—and then I realize I’ve been focused on some tiny side issue and the main decision didn’t get made clearly.\n\nAnd with the lab, it’s… I don’t want to sound careless, because I’ve always been careful. But the missed priorities and confused scheduling, and me not supervising people the way I should, it started putting the project at risk, and the safety expectations too. So yes, it’s definitely affecting my confidence. I feel like people are watching for where I’m going to drop the ball now.\n\nDoctor: When you get caught up in these details at work or at home, do you find it extremely difficult to switch your attention away from them, even if you know you should focus on something else or if it's affecting your responsibilities?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s exactly it, I think. I know I should stop, and I’ll even tell myself, “No, this is not the point right now,” but then I can’t let go of it.\n\nLike in a grant call, everyone’s talking about timelines or money, and I’ll notice the pump vibration in the lab sounds just slightly wrong—except I’m not even supposed to be thinking about that right then—and suddenly I’m mentally gone. Or a student says something small, almost nothing, and I’m chasing that thread. Or I remember some odd peak from an old spectrum and it feels urgent, like I have to figure it out right now.\n\nAnd then hours can go by. The actual experiment, or the decision people needed from me, just… falls apart because I couldn’t switch back. It’s embarrassing, because I can see it happening, but seeing it doesn’t seem to make me able to stop.\n\nDoctor: Can you recall a time when you were so focused on a specific detail or task that you lost track of time or failed to notice what was going on around you, even when there were other important things happening that required your attention?\n\nPatient: Yes. The grant call is probably the cleanest example, although “clean” is the wrong word because it wasn’t clean at all.\n\nEveryone was talking about timelines and what we could realistically deliver, and I was supposed to be tracking the experiment plan, but I noticed this pump vibration in the background. It was just slightly different. And then I couldn’t leave it alone. I started thinking about whether it matched something from a previous run, and then that reminded me of an odd peak in an old spectrum, and then I was digging through that in my head instead of listening.\n\nBy the time I sort of came back, I realized I’d missed what people were asking me. Hours can go like that. A student can make some small side comment, or I see one detail that feels wrong, and suddenly I’m chasing that while the thing everyone actually needed from me just falls apart. It’s awful, because it isn’t that I don’t know there are more important things happening. I do know. I just can’t seem to stay with them.\n\nDoctor: When you're stuck focusing on a minor detail, like the pump vibration or an old spectrum peak, do you find it nearly impossible to shift your mind to a different task or topic, even when you're aware that you need to?\n\nPatient: Yes. Nearly impossible is… honestly, that’s what it feels like.\n\nI came in because I can’t keep my mind on the actual experiment anymore. I’ll be in a grant call or a safety meeting, and I know I’m supposed to be following the main issue, but then I notice the pump vibration, or a student makes some side comment, or I remember an odd peak from an old spectrum, and suddenly my brain just goes there.\n\nAnd it’s not a quick thought. It turns into this whole chase, like I have to solve that one thing right then, even though everyone else needs me on the actual decision. Hours can go by, and then the important thing has fallen apart. It makes me feel like I’m not in control of my own attention.\n\nDoctor: Has this pattern of getting sidetracked by small details caused problems in your relationships or other areas of your life beyond work, such as with family, friends, or in social situations?\n\nPatient: Yes… mostly it’s shown up through work, but it spills over because work is where most of my relationships are, if that makes sense. My department has had to step in on decisions I used to handle myself. Not just small things—priorities I missed, scheduling getting confused, supervision with students getting strained.\n\nAnd then it becomes awkward with people, because they’re trying to trust me and I’m off chasing some tiny detail, and meanwhile the project plan is slipping. There were also lab safety expectations that people started worrying I wasn’t keeping on top of, which is… that’s hard to say out loud.\n\nI mean, I used to be the person people came to for those decisions. Now there’s this feeling that others have to check behind me or take over before something important gets missed. That has definitely affected how people relate to me.\n\nDoctor: When you get stuck on these small details, like the pump vibration or the old spectrum peak, do you feel like you have to keep thinking about them over and over, almost like you can't stop unless you figure them out completely, even if you know they're not that important?\n\nPatient: Yes, that’s… that’s pretty much it. It isn’t just that I notice the thing and move on. I’ll be in a grant call or a safety meeting, and I know the actual experiment plan is what matters, but then there’s this pump vibration, or someone says something offhand, or I remember that old spectrum peak, and my mind just grabs it.\n\nAnd then I keep circling it. Like if I can just figure out why it sounds different, or what that peak meant, then I can come back—but I don’t come back. Hours can disappear, and meanwhile the thing everyone needed from me is falling apart. It feels ridiculous because part of me knows it’s not the most important thing right then, but I still can’t seem to stop chasing it.\n\nDoctor: When you get stuck on these small details like the pump vibration or an old spectrum peak, do you feel like you have to keep thinking about them over and over, almost like you can't stop unless you figure them out completely, even if you know they're not that important?\n\nPatient: Yes, and I know I already keep coming back to that, but that’s exactly the problem—I come in because I can’t keep my mind on the actual experiment anymore.\n\nLike during a grant call, or a safety meeting, everyone’s talking about something important, and then I hear the pump vibrating differently, or a student says one little side thing, or I remember some odd peak in an old spectrum from months ago, and suddenly that’s where I am. I’m not really in the room anymore, mentally.\n\nAnd I tell myself, okay, just note it and come back, but I don’t. I start chasing it, turning it over, trying to make it make sense, and then hours can go by. Meanwhile the thing everyone actually needed me for is just… falling apart. It’s embarrassing, because I know it shouldn’t matter that much right then, but it feels like I can’t let it go until I’ve figured it out.\n\nDoctor: When you're working on a task or problem that really interests you, do you find yourself becoming so absorbed in it that you lose track of time or neglect other things, even if they’re important?\n\nPatient: Yes, but it’s not always the useful kind of absorbed. I mean, if it’s something that catches me, I can disappear into it for hours. Like I came in because I can’t keep my mind on the actual experiment anymore.\n\nI’ll be in a grant call or a safety meeting, and instead of following what people need from me, I’ll notice the pump vibration is different, or a student makes some little side comment, or I remember this odd peak from an old spectrum, and then I’m just… gone. I start chasing that one thing, trying to figure it out.\n\nAnd then hours pass, and the actual task—the experiment plan, the decision, whatever everyone was waiting on—has fallen apart. It’s not like I’m choosing the important thing and focusing well. It’s more like my attention latches onto the wrong thing and won’t let go.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any problems with your mood, sleep, or general sense of well-being that aren't just related to the stress at work?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, it’s not just work stress, though work is where everyone sees it. For months I’ve had this feeling like the lab is finally moving at my speed, like everything is sort of charged or electrified, if that makes sense.\n\nAnd when people keep up with me, it feels great—like finally, yes, this is how fast it should have been going. But if someone questions the plan, or asks me to slow down and go back over something, I get… sharp. Too sharp. I snap at them. I’ve noticed students don’t come by my office the way they used to, which is embarrassing, because I don’t think I’m trying to scare them. It just feels intolerable in the moment, like they’re putting brakes on something that’s finally working.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your thoughts racing, or feeling like you need to keep talking because your mind is moving so fast?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it racing, exactly. I mean… people have said I’m hard to follow sometimes, but that could just be because I’m trying to explain the whole picture and they want the short version.\n\nThe talking thing—maybe? I don’t know. In meetings I do feel like if I don’t get the thought out right then, it’ll branch into five other things and we’ll lose the important part. But then I start explaining one piece, and it reminds me of another piece, like when we were setting up the instrument schedule and someone mislabeled the sample queue—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nI guess I notice my mind jumping around, but whether it’s “racing,” I don’t know. It feels more like there’s just too much connected at once.\n\nDoctor: When you get caught up in these small details like the pump vibration or old spectrum peaks, do you ever feel like it makes perfect sense to you even if others might not understand why it’s so important?\n\nPatient: Yes—at the time it feels completely sensible. Like, if the pump vibration changes, that could mean something about the setup, or contamination, or a timing issue, and suddenly that feels more urgent than whatever the meeting was supposed to be about.\n\nBut then I look up and I’ve lost hours. The grant call is over, or the safety decision didn’t get made, or people are waiting for me to sign off on the actual experiment plan. And I know from the outside it probably looks like I’m fixated on some tiny irrelevant thing, but in my head it feels like the thread everything else depends on.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had difficulty maintaining relationships or keeping up with daily responsibilities, even in situations that don’t involve work or scientific tasks?\n\nPatient: I don’t know. I mean, outside of work it’s harder to measure, isn’t it? Like no one is grading whether I remembered to call someone back or whether the laundry got done in the correct order—although I do have opinions about the correct order, because towels take forever and then everything smells damp if you misjudge it.\n\nRelationships… I’m not sure. I don’t think of myself as someone who can’t maintain them, but people do get tired, maybe. Or I get tired of explaining myself. I’m sorry, I’m not sure I’m answering what you asked. Can you say what kind of responsibilities you mean?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
